Normal value and export price from China PR. 


Normal value for China PR 
Article 5 of China’s Accession Protocol in WTO provides as follows: 


Article VI of the GATT 7994, the Agreement on Implementation of Article VI of the 
General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade /994 (“Anti-Dumping Agreement’’) and the 
SCM Agreement shall apply in proceedings involving imports of Chinese origin into a 
WTO Member consistent with the following: 

(a) In determining price comparability under Article VI of the GATT 7994 and the 
Anti-Dumping Agreement, the importing WTO Member shall use either Chinese prices 
or costs for the industry under investigation or a methodology that is not based on a 
strict comparison with domestic prices or costs in China based on the following rules: 


(i) If the producers under investigation can clearly show that market economy 
conditions prevail in the industry producing the like product with regard to the 
manufacture, production and sale of that product, the importing WTO Member shall 
use Chinese prices or costs for the industry under investigation in determining price 
comparability; 


(ii) The importing WO Member may use a methodology that is not based on a strict 
comparison with domestic prices or costs in China if the producers under investigation 
cannot clearly show that market economy conditions prevail in the industry producing 
the like product with regard to manufacture, production and sale of that product. 


(b) In proceedings under Parts IT, III and V of the SCM Agreement, when addressing 
subsidies described in Articles /4(6), /4(6), /4(०) and /4(9), relevant provisions of the 
SCM Agreement shall apply; however, if there are special difficulties in that 
application, the importing WTO member may then use methodologies for identifying 
and measuring the subsidy benefit which take into account the possibility that 
prevailing terms and conditions in China may not always be available as appropriate 
benchmarks. In applying such methodologies, where practicable, the importing WTO 
Member should adjust such prevailing terms and conditions before considering the use 
of terms and conditions prevailing outside China. 


(c) The importing WTO Member shall notify methodologies used in accordance with 
subparagraph (a) to the Committee on Anti-Dumping Practices and shall notify 
methodologies used in accordance with subparagraph (b) to the Committee on 
Subsidies and Countervailing Measures. 


[भाग [खण्ड ] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 9] 


49. 


50. 


a 


(d) Once China has established, under the national law of the importing WTO 
Member, that it is a market economy, the provisions of subparagraph (a) shall be 
terminated provided that the importing Member's national law contains market 
economy criteria as of the date of accession. In any event, the provision of 
subparagraph (a)(ii) shall expire ॥| years after the date of accession. In addition, 
should China establish, pursuant to the national law of the importing WTO member, 
that market economy conditions prevail in a particular industry or sector, the 
nonmarket economy provisions of subparagraph (a) shall no longer apply to that 
industry or sector." 


The applicants had relied upon Article |5(a)(i) of China’s the Accession Protocol as well as 
para 7 of the Annexure I and claimed that producers in China PR must be asked to 
demonstrate that market economy conditions prevail in their industry producing the like 
product with regard to the manufacture, production and sale of the product under 
consideration. It has been stated by the applicants that in case the responding Chinese 
producers are not able to demonstrate that their costs and price information are market- 
driven, the normal value should be calculated in terms of provisions of Para 7 and 8 of 
Annexure- I to the Rules. 


It is noted that while the provision contained in Section I5 (a)(ii) has expired on .2.206, 
the provision under Article 2.2.!.l of WTO Anti-dumping Agreement read with the 
obligation under Section |5(a)(i) of the Accession Protocol require criterion stipulated in 
paragraph 8 of Annexure I of the Rules to be satisfied through the information/data to be 
provided in the supplementary questionnaire on claiming market economy treatment. It is 
noted that since the responding producers/exporters from China PR have not submitted 
response to the supplementary questionnaire the normal value computation is required to be 
done as per the provisions of paragraph 7 of Annexure I of the Rules. 


As none of the producers from China PR have claimed determination of normal value on the 
basis of their own data/information, the normal value has been determined in accordance 
with paragraph 7 of Annexure I of the Rules, which reads as under: 


“7. In case of imports from non-market economy countries, normal value shall be 
determined on the basis of the price or constructed value in a market economy third 
country, or the price from such a third country to other countries, including India, or 
where it is not possible, on any other reasonable basis, including the price actually 
paid or payable in India for the like product, duly adjusted if necessary, to include a 
reasonable profit margin. An appropriate market economy third country shall be 
selected by the designated authority in a reasonable manner [keeping in view the level 
of development of the country concerned and the product in question and due account 
shall be taken of any reliable information made available at the time of the selection. 
Account shall also be taken within time limits; where appropriate, of the investigation 
if any made in similar matter in respect of any other market economy third country. 
The parties to the investigation shall be informed without unreasonable delay the 


92 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SECc.]] 


52. 


53. 


54. 


55. 


56. 


aforesaid selection of the market economy third country and shall be given a 
reasonable period of time to offer their comments.” 


Para 7 lays down a hierarchy for the determination of normal value and provides that normal 
value shall be determined on the basis of price or constructed value in a market economy 
third country, or the price from such a third country to any other country, including India, or 
where it is not possible, on any reasonable basis, including the price actually paid or payable 
in India for the like article, duly adjusted, if necessary, to include a reasonable profit margin. 
Thus, the Authority notes that the normal value is required to be determined having regard to 
the various sequential alternatives provided under Para 7. 


The Authority also notes the existing jurisprudence on constructing the normal value in the 
case of a non-market economy contained in the various judgements. These judgements 
provide directions regarding the implementation of Para 7 of Annexure I of the Rules 
concerning the choice of an appropriate option, and associated obligations thereof. 


At the stage of application, the applicants have claimed normal value on the basis of price 
from such third country to other countries including India. For this purpose, the applicants 
had considered the import price from Turkey into India. 


As per the legal hierarchy, the normal value should be determined based on either the price 
or the constructed value in a market economy third country. If such information is 
unavailable, it should then be based on export prices from that country to other destinations, 
including India. The Authority notes that interested parties have proposed Thailand as a 
surrogate country for determining the normal value for China. However, the anti-dumping 
rules specify that a suitable market economy country should be selected considering the 
level of development of the country in question and the nature of the product. No evidence 
has been provided to demonstrate that China and Thailand are at comparable levels of 
development. Furthermore, Thailand has only one producer, whereas China has many, and 
Thailand’s domestic demand is minimal. Thus, the level of development of the product in 
China and Thailand are not comparable. The interested parties have also cited a previous 
anti-dumping investigation involving imports of Chlorinated Polyvinyl Chloride (CPVC) 
Resin from China and Korea, where Thailand was used as a market economy country. The 
Authority clarifies that in that case, normal value for China was determined based on 
Thailand’s export prices and not its domestic prices. It was specifically noted that Thailand’s 
export volume was the highest among non-subject countries, and since there were no anti- 
dumping measures in place against Thailand, the CIF price was deemed representative. 
Therefore, the Authority holds that Thailand cannot be regarded as a surrogate country for 
China in the current case. 


The next methodology under the rules is to consider export prices from a market economy 
third country to other countries, including India. Among non-subject countries, Turkey has 
the highest import volume. Since Turkey is not subject to any anti-dumping measures and 
the volume of its exports exceeds the de minimis threshold, and those exports are not 
dumped, it qualifies under this method. The Authority also refers to the decision in Kuitun 


[भाग [खण्ड ] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 93 


57. 


ii. 


56. 


59. 


60. 


6l. 


62. 


63. 


Jinjiang Chemical Industry Co. Ltd. vs. Union of India, where the Hon’ble CESTAT held 
that when normal value is determined based on exports from a market economy third 
country to India, the relevant factors are the volume of such exports and the absence of 
dumping during the period of investigation. Therefore, the Authority has considered normal 
value based on the import price from Turkey, duly adjusted to arrive at the ex-factory level.

Normal value. 

To determine the normal value, the Authority has conducted the ordinary course of trade test 
to determine profit-making domestic sales transactions with reference to the cost of 
production of the subject goods on a PCN-wise basis. Where for PCN, less than 80% of 
sales were made at profit, the normal value has been determined based on the selling price of 
profitable sales. For the PCNs, where ever more than 80% of sales were made at profit, the 
normal value has been determined based on the selling price of all sales of that PCN.

७. EXAMINATION OF INJURY AND CAUSAL LINK 
G. l Submission made by the other interested parties 
76. The other interested parties have made the following submissions with regard to injury and 


causal link: 

i. Demand in India has fallen from ,04,l!9 MT in the base year to 96,29] in the period 
of investigation. On the contrary, the sales of the domestic industry have remained 
stable throughout the injury period. 

ii. | Any increase in the volume of allegedly dumped imports from subject countries is due 
to the decrease in market share of imports from other countries. 

iii. The imports from Thailand have remained stable. There is a sharp increase in imports 
from China PR (250%) which is the source of any injury to the domestic industry. 

iv. The imports from Thailand and China have increased by 248 and 58 index points 
respectively but imports from Peru have only increased by | index points in the period 
of investigation as compared to base year. 

v. The imports from Peru have remained stable over time and have not impacted the 
prices and market share of the applicants. 

vi. The injury margins for Thailand are in the range of 0-0%, which is not significant 
enough to warrant anti-dumping duties. They maintained that their pricing strategies 
have been fair and competitive, without engaging in aggressive undercutting. 

vil. A thorough analysis of domestic industry data shows that there is no significant 
volume or price effects due to the alleged dumped imports. Overall demand for the 
product has declined, and factors such as fire-related stock loss and pandemic-related 
disruptions must be considered. 

vill. The acrylic fibre market has been impacted by rising acrylonitrile prices. This has 
possessed significant challenges on the manufacturer to maintain profitability. The 
same has been confirmed by Pasupati in its annual report of 2023-24. 

ix. Conditions of competition between imports from Peru and imports from the other 
subject countries are clearly different. The imports should therefore not be cumulated. 

x. | The import price has increased by 30% in the period of investigation from the base 
year but the applicants’ selling price has remained stable. The applicants could have 
increased selling prices but due to strategic business decision, it decided to not 
increase the prices. 

xl. The import price from Thailand is higher than the other countries. Thus, any price 
pressure on the applicants is due to low priced imports from other countries.

low-capacity utilization, and high costs, rather than alleged dumped imports. 
Vardhman in their annual report had admitted that high acrylonitrile consumption 
costs due to high-priced stock during the period of investigation contributed to the 
decline in demand.

G.2 Submission made by the applicants

The applicants have made the following submissions with regard to injury and causal link 

i. The product has been repeatedly dumped into India for more than two decades. The 
shift of dumping from one country to another has not allowed the applicants to operate 
at reasonable profits. 

ii. | There is only one producer in Thailand, namely Thai Acrylic Limited (TAF). TAF 
alone has a long history of dumping the product in the Indian market. 

ili. Since the original investigation in 997, the Authority has repeatedly found TAF is

Thailand have continued to rise. TAF modus operandi involves dumping the product 
to capture the market.

Thailand continuously increasing trend from early 999- 00 to 20I5-l6. This was the 
period when there was no demand supply gap.

increase in domestic consumption, TAF increased its capacity. At present, TAF have 
an installed capacity of ,20,000 MT against domestic demand of mere 5000 MT. It is

and largely considering Chinese market.

from Thailand declined more than the decline in the cost. TAF has sold the product at

Examination by the Authority 


The Authority has taken note of various submissions made by the applicants and other 
interested parties on injury and causal link and has analyzed the same, considering the facts 
available on record in light of applicable laws. The injury analysis made by the Authority 
addresses the various submissions made by the applicants and other interested parties. 


As regards the submission that the domestic industry has procured acrylonitrile at higher 
prices which has led to high costs, it is clarified that the Authority has relied upon 
information from the books of accounts. The same is consistent with the practice of the 


[भाग aus ]] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 0] 


80. 


8. 


82. 


83. 


Authority. The cost of sales is calculated based on the consumption price of the raw material 
for the entire year. 


As regards submission on statements in the annual reports stating that tough competition 
from natural fibre and a decline in demand for the product have been a cause of injury, the 
Authority notes that statements made in the annual report pertain to the company’s overall 
performance, and presents macro-economic situation relating to the products. The 
performance may not be representative of the performance of the product under 
consideration during the investigation period and how imports have impacted the domestic 
industry. Further, it is seen that the demand or consumption for the product has declined. 
However, despite the same, the imports from subject countries have increased. Thus, 
increase in imports at dumped price when demand has fallen itself establishes that dumping 
has caused material injury to the domestic industry. It is also seen that one of the applicants 
(Indian Acrylics Limited) has identified, in its annual report, low priced imports as a cause of 
injury. It is also relevant that the Authority is concerned with margin of dumping and not 
reasons for dumping, while annual reports may explain these reasons for benefit of the 
shareholders. 


As regards submission on an increase in the capital employed without an increase in the 
capacity, the Authority notes that changes in the capital employed are attributable to changes 
in the working capital deployed by the domestic industry. The net fixed assets deployed by 
the domestic industry have in fact declined. The Authority also notes that an increase in 
capital employed need not be limited to an increase in the capacity. The increase in the 
capital employed may be due to a number of factors such as a change in plant and 
machinery, an increase in the working capital, etc. In the present case, the capital employed 
has increased because the working capital of the domestic industry has increased which has 
led to an increase in the capital employed. 


Regarding the submission that only Indian Acrylics Limited has incurred losses while the 
other two applicants have shown profits, the Authority notes that, in accordance with Rule 
(2) of the Rules, the assessment of injury must be based on the domestic industry as a 
whole, rather than on individual companies. Since the domestic industry has been defined to 
include Indian Acrylics Limited and Pasupati Acrylon Limited, the injury analysis has been 
conducted collectively for these two producers. Moreover, even when the arguments of the 
other interested are considered, it is seen from the information provided by them that both 
Pasupati Acrylon Limited and Vardhaman Acrylics Limited have also experienced a steep 
decline in profits. 


It has been stated by the interested parties and the applicants that the performance was also 
impacted by Covid-l9. The Authority notes that the Covid conditions prevailed during the 
year 2020-2! and 202l-22. The applicants too have requested that a comparison may be 
made with 2022-23. The Authority notes that the purpose of determining a period of 
investigation and the complete injury period for injury assessment is to consider the 
performance of the domestic industry for the entire period of four years. Therefore, the 
Authority has compared the performance of the domestic industry from the base year. 


02 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SECc.]] 


84. 


85. 


86. 


87. 


88. 


However, performance in the intervening period has been given due weightage in injury 
analysis. 


Cumulative Assessment 


Para (iii) of Annexure II of the Anti-Dumping Rules provides that in case where imports of a 
product from more than one country are being simultaneously subjected to anti- dumping 
investigation. the Authority will cumulatively assess the effect of such imports, in case it 
determines that: 


a. The margin of dumping established in relation to the imports from each country is more 
than two percent expressed as a percentage of export price and the volume of the 
imports from each country is three percent (or more) of the import of like article or 
where the export of individual countries is less than three percent, the imports 
collectively account for more than seven percent of the import of like article, and 

b. | Cumulative assessment of the effect of imports is appropriate in light of the conditions 
of competition between the imported article and the like domestic articles. 


The Authority notes that: 


a. The subject goods are being dumped into India from the subject countries. The margins 
of dumping from each of the subject countries are more than the de-minimis limits 
prescribed under the Rules. 

b. | The volume of imports from each of the subject countries is individually more than 3% 
of the total volume of imports. 

c. Cumulative assessments of the effects of imports are appropriate as the exports from the 
subject countries not only directly compete with the like articles offered by each of 
them but also the like articles offered by the applicants in the Indian market. 


With regard to the contention that imports from Peru should not be cumulatively assessed 
due to differences in competitive conditions between imports from Peru and imports from 
other subject countries, the Authority notes that the information on record shows that users 
are procuring the product under consideration from Peru and other subject countries. It is 
also seen that the price of the product under consideration from Peru and other subject 
countries has moved in tandem. Therefore, the Authority does not find it appropriate to 
decumulate the imports from Peru and other subject countries. 


In view of the above, the Authority considers that it is appropriate to assess injury to the 
domestic industry cumulatively from imports of the subject goods from the subject 
countries. 


Rule ll of the Anti-Dumping Rules read with Annexure II provides that an injury 
determination shall involve examination of factors that may indicate injury to the domestic 
industry, “... taking into account all relevant facts, including the volume of dumped imports, 
their effect on prices in the domestic market for like articles and the consequent effect of 


[भाग [खण्ड ] 


भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 03 


such imports on domestic producers of such articles..”. In considering the effect of the 
dumped imports on prices, it is considered necessary to examine whether there has been a 
significant price undercutting by the dumped imports as compared with the price of the like 
article in India, or whether the effect of such imports is otherwise to depress prices to a 
significant degree or prevent price increases, which otherwise would have occurred, to a 
significant degree. For the examination of the impact of the dumped imports on the domestic 
industry in India, indices having a bearing on the state of the industry such as production, 
capacity utilization, sales volume, inventory, profitability, net sales realization, the 
magnitude and margin of dumping, etc. have been considered in accordance with Annexure 
II of the anti-dumping rules.

of the dumped imports from the subject countries with reference to the price undercutting 
and price suppression/ depression, if any. For the purpose of this analysis, the cost of 
production and the selling price of the domestic industry have been compared with the 
landed price of the imports of the subject goods from the subject countries.

H.4.4 Economic parameters of the domestic industry. 


05. 


06. 


Annexure II to the Anti-Dumping Rules provide that the examination of the impact of the 
dumped imports on the domestic industry should include an objective and unbiased 
evaluation of all the relevant economic factors and indices having a bearing on the state of 
the industry, including actual and potential decline in the sales, profits, output, market share, 
productivity, return on investments or utilization of capacity; factors affecting domestic 
prices, the magnitude of the margin of the dumping; actual and potential negative effects on 
the cash flow, inventories, employment, wages, growth and the ability to raise the capital 
investments. The various injury parameters relating to the domestic industry are discussed 
below. The Authority has examined the injury parameters objectively taking into account 
various facts and arguments made by the interested parties in their submissions:

The profits of the domestic industry sharply declined in 202-22 when subject imports 
increased despite decline in demand. Profits increased in 2022-23 when the demand 
increased, but subject imports remained at similar level. The domestic industry was

With significant increase in dumped imports in the period of investigation, despite 
decline in demand, the profits of the domestic industry declined sharply and domestic 
industry suffered financial losses. 

The cash profit and PBIT followed the same trend as that of profit before tax. Cash 
profit and PBIT declined in 202-22, increased in 2022-23 and then steeply declined in 
the period of investigation. The domestic industry is operating with cash losses and has 
the lowest PBIT in the period of investigation. 

The return on capital investment declined sharply in the period of investigation as 
compared to the previous year. The domestic industry has recorded their lowest return 
during the period of investigation.

It is seen that the domestic industry has recorded negative growth in volume and price 
parameters in the period of investigation. With increase in low priced imports from subject 
countries in the POI, the growth of the domestic industry in production, domestic sales, 
capacity utilization, profit per unit, PBIT per unit, cash profit per unit and return on capital 
employed was negative. 


Magnitude of dumping margin 

The magnitude of dumping is an indicator of the extent to which the imports are being 
dumping into India. The investigation has shown that the dumping margin is positive and 
significant during the period of investigation. 


Ability to raise capital investment. 

The performance of the domestic industry was adversely impacted on both volume and price 
accounts. As the import price from subject countries declined, the domestic industry suffered 
from losses, cash losses, and low return on investment. Thus, the ability of the domestic 
industry to raise capital investment has been significantly impacted. 


Factors affecting price 

Examination of DG system import data showed that the weighted average import price from 
subject countries is below the selling price of the domestic industry. With increase in the 
volume of imports from subject countries, the domestic industry has been unable to change 
its selling price commensurate with the changes in the cost of sales. There is significant price 
effect of the imports due to low priced imports from subject countries. Therefore, the imports 
from subject countries have affected the prices of the applicants. 


Conclusion on injury 
Based on the above, the following conclusions have been drawn. 


a. Volume of imports from subject countries has increased in absolute terms as well as in relation 
to production and consumption. 


2 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.]] 


22. 


23: 


24. 


25. 


b. Despite having sufficient capacities with the domestic industry, the imports from subject 
countries have increased. 

८. The subject imports are undercutting the prices of the domestic industry. The domestic 
industry has not been able to move its prices in line with the movement in the cost of 
production and therefore, domestic industry’s prices are depressed. 

d. Capacity of the domestic industry remained constant over the injury period. Production, 
capacity utilisation and domestic sales of the domestic industry have declined. 

6. Due to dumped imports, the domestic industry was forced to shut down its operation. 

f. | The market share of subject imports has increased in the period of investigation while that of 
the domestic industry have declined. The market share of Indian industry has also declined in 
the period of investigation. 

g. The average inventories with the domestic industry increased in the period of investigation. 

h. The domestic industry is suffering financial losses. The cash profit turned negative and profit 
before interest declined in the period of investigation. The domestic industry has recorded their 
lowest return in the period of investigation. 

i. The domestic industry has seen negative growth both in volume and price parameters. 

i The dumping margin is positive and significant. 

k. The dumping from the subject countries has impacted the ability of the domestic industry to 
raise capital investment. 

ll The weighted average landed price of the dumped imports has declined steeply and is the only 
factor affecting the prices of the domestic industry. 


The Authority concludes that the domestic industry has suffered material injury. 
CAUSAL LINK AND NON-ATTRIBUTION ANALYSIS 


The Authority is required to examine any known factors other than dumped imports which 
are injuring the domestic industry, so that the injury caused by these other factors may not be 
attributed to the dumped imports. Factors which may be relevant in this respect include, inter 
alia, the volume and prices of imports not sold at dumped prices, contraction in demand or 
changes in the patterns of consumption, trade restrictive practices of and competition 
between the foreign and the domestic producers, developments in technology and the export 
performance and the productivity of the applicants. It has been examined below whether the 
factors listed under the Rules could have contributed to the injury suffered by the applicants. 


Volume and price of imports from third countries 

Imports from the subject countries constitute 74% share in the total imports into India. The 
imports of the subject goods from other countries are either (a) at insignificant volumes, or 
(b) at higher prices and hence not causing injury to the domestic industry. Further, whereas 
the subject imports have increased significantly, imports from other countries have steeply 
declined. 


Contraction in demand 

The demand for the product under consideration has declined in the period of investigation 
and over the injury period. Despite a decline in demand, the imports from subject countries 
have increased. Resultantly, the decline in domestic sales of the domestic industry is far 


[भाग eve ]] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 3 


28. 


I3 I: 


}32, 


higher than the decline in demand. With the decline in demand, both domestic industry and 
subject imports should have shown a decline. Whereas the demand declined by around 8000 
MT, the subject imports increased by 6000 MT and the Indian industry lost sales by 4,000 
MT. Even other domestic producers also lost sales volumes. Hence, decline in demand could 
not have been the principal cause of injury to the domestic industry. 


Change in the pattern of consumption 


. There has been no known material change in the pattern of consumption for the product 


under consideration. 


Trade restrictive practices 


. No interested parties have provided any evidence relating to any known trade restrictive 


practice, which could have caused injury to the domestic industry. Therefore, the Authority 
notes that trade restrictive practice has not caused injury to the domestic industry. 


Development of technology 

The Authority notes that the technology for the production of the subject goods has not 
undergone any change. Hence, development in technology has not caused injury to the 
domestic industry. 


Export performance 


. The Authority has considered the injury data for the domestic operations only for the injury 


analysis. Therefore, export performance is not the cause of injury to the domestic industry. 


Performance of other products 

The Authority has considered data relating to the performance of the subject goods only. 
Therefore, the performance of the other products produced and sold by the domestic industry 
is not a possible cause of injury. 


Causal link between dumping and injury. 

While other known factors listed under the Rules have not caused injury to the domestic 

industry, the Authority notes that the following parameters show that injury to the domestic 

industry is caused by the dumped imports. 

a.Imports from subject countries are at dumped prices. 

b. The landed price of imports is below the cost and price of the domestic industry. 

c.The positive price undercutting and price suppression has adversely impacted the profits, cash 
profits and return on investment of the applicants. 

d. As a result of low-priced imports, the domestic industry’s profitability turned into losses 
during the period of investigation. 

e.With an increase in dumped imports, the domestic sales of the domestic industry have declined. 
Consequently, production and market share of the domestic industry has declined. 


The Authority, thus, concludes that there exists a causal link between the dumping of the 
subject goods and injury to the domestic industry.

THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEC.]]

34. 


The Authority has determined the non-injurious price for the applicants on the basis of 
principles laid down in the Rules read with Annexure III, as amended. The non-injurious 
price has been determined by adopting the information/data relating to the cost of production 
provided by the applicants. The non-injurious price has been compared with the landed price 
of the product under consideration from the subject countries for calculating the injury 
margin. For determining the non-injurious price, the best utilization of the raw materials and 
utilities and the best utilization of production capacity has been considered. Extraordinary or 
non-recurring expenses and/or assets have been excluded from the cost of production and/or 
non-injurious price. A reasonable return (pre-tax @ 22%) on average capital employed (i.e., 
average net fixed assets plus average working capital) deployed for the product under 
consideration has been allowed for recovery of interest, corporate tax, and profit to arrive at 
the non-injurious price as prescribed in Annexure III of the Rules. 


The landed price and non-injurious price determined as above have been compared on PCN 
basis. The weighted average of the injury margin determined for producers/exporters by the 
Authority is provided in the table below:

producing sweaters and shawls, employing around |0 lakh people. These industries

The past duties had allowed the applicant to earn super abnormal profits. 

The past duties on the product have historically increased yarn prices, making India’s 
downstream industries like sweater/shawl production uncompetitive compared to 
countries like China, Vietnam, and Bangladesh. This has impacted both domestic and

constitutes 60-70% of the cost of the downstream users and the anti-dumping duty

cr. The import value over the injury period is more than Rs 000 cr.

to Rs ,50,000 per MT. The past high import prices did not affect the user industry. 
Thus, it is very unlikely that the anti-dumping measures will impact them.

Producers from the subject countries operate solely on revenue maximization. They 
may switch markets for better prices. On the other hand, the applicants, being in the 
same national territory as consumers, will prioritize the consumer's interest. 

The applicants have undertaken various social activities from the profit earned from 
this business. If the duties are not imposed, profits of the applicants will decline 
affecting all the initiatives taken by the applicants. Anti-dumping duty is in the interest 
of producers, consumers and public at large. 

Anti-dumping measures is not a protection but a tool to address the unfair pricing in 
the Indian market. Rather, the purpose of anti-dumping duty is to prevent unfair trade 
practice from producer’s exporters from subject countries. 

On the submission that the spinning mills employ large number of people, the spinning 
mills are not working in acrylic fibre exclusively. Acrylic fibre is only one of the 
products for them. 

Import prices from Thailand fluctuated significantly over the injury period, allowing 
consumers to swiftly react to price changes. In contrast, the price changes due to 
proposed ADD would be insignificant, and consumers could easily pass on this 
increase in price. 

The sale price of yarn moved in tandem with the purchase price of fibre, indicates that 
the consumers have the ability to align their sales prices accordingly. 

The domestic industry operated with idle capacities; thus, the users won't face supply 
disruption. The domestic industry would be able to supply to the extent of full import 
volume, if operated at 88% capacity. 

There are various other sources of supply in addition to the domestic industry. The 
domestic industry doesn't seek import prohibition but fair, un-dumped prices, ensuring 
no supply shortage for consumers. 

Vardhman Acrylic Ltd. (VAL), which produces fibre, operates independently from 
Vardhman Textiles Ltd. (VTL), which consumes fibre. The sales made by VAL to 
VTL are at arm’s length and at market price. VTL's market position remains 
competitive with other yarn consumers. Fibre and yarn production are distinct 
activities within the Group. There is no reason why group should resort to cross 
subsidization of fibre to the yarn company. 

An 80% duty on Peru does not mean an 80% increase in fibre prices. Even a 5% duty 
wouldn't have a 4% impact on yarn prices, considering yarn constitutes 60% of the 
cost. 

Financial results of Ganga Agrowools Limited shows that consumers can absorb a 3- 
4% cost increase. Historically, yarn and fibre prices move together. Yarn consumers 
would pass fibre price to the downstream product. 

Yarn costs form a 60-80% in the downstream products; hence a 3-4% increase in yarn 
prices would result in about a 2% increase in garment prices, easily absorbed by 
consumers. 

While imports of downstream products attract a 20% basic customs duty, Acrylic 
Fibre has only a 5% duty. The downstream industries are already protected through 
basic custom duty. 

Even if the basic custom duty on yarn is reduced, yarn industry would be able to 
absorb the same.

J.3. Examination by the Authority 


37. The Authority considered whether imposition of the proposed anti-dumping duty will be 
against public interest. This determination is based on consideration of information on 


l8 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEC.]]

4].

records and interests of various parties including the domestic industry, foreign producers 
and consumers. 


The Authority issued gazette notification inviting views from all the interested parties, 
including importers, consumers and other interested parties. The Authority also prescribed a 
questionnaire for the users to provide the relevant information with regard to the present 
investigation, including possible effect of the anti-dumping duty on their operation. The 
Authority sought information on, inter-alia, interchangeability of the product supplied by the 
various suppliers from different countries, ability to switch sources, the effect of the anti- 
dumping duty on the consumers, the factors that are likely to accelerate or delay the 
adjustment to the new situation caused by the imposition of the anti-dumping duty. 


The Authority notes that the purpose of anti-dumping duty, in general is to eliminate injury 
caused to the domestic industry by the unfair trade practices of dumping so as to re-establish 
a situation of open and fair competition in the Indian market, which is in the general interest 
of the country. 


The Authority had prescribed an economic interest questionnaire which was sent to all 
interested parties in this investigation. Apart from the applicants, participating users have 
filed the questionnaire. However, only applicants have quantified the impact of proposed 
measures. The Authority recognizes that imposition of anti-dumping measures may lead to 
increase in the prices. However, there was significant decline in the prices of the product in 
last three years. The prices would still remain materially below the past prices. Further, anti- 
dumping duty ensures that a level playing field is maintained between the foreign exporters 
and the domestic industry. 


As regards submission that past duties on the product led to increase in yarn prices, the 
Authority notes that there is no information on record to suggest that there was any adverse 
impact on the downstream industry as a result of the previously duties in force. The domestic 
industry submitted that the yarn prices move in tandem with the fibre prices, and therefore 
impact should be determined on the eventual end product. In so far as eventual end product 
is concerned, the domestic industry submitted that the impact of proposed anti-dumping on 
eventual end product would be quite insignificant.

It has been stated by the users that they operate on wafer-thin margins. However,

[भाग [खण्ड ]] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 9

that the users have reported adequate profits. While the users have reported profits, the 
domestic industry has suffered losses. 


As regards submission that the imposition of anti-dumping duties would unfairly penalize 
Indian users who rely on high-quality or specialized acrylic fibre from these sources. The 
Authority notes that the anti-dumping measures will not restrict imports from the subject 
countries in any manner. Further, apart from the domestic industry and subject countries, 
there are imports from other countries as well. The Indian industry has the capacity to meet 
the entire demand in India. Even if the users continue to source the product from the subject 
countries, they are free to import after payment of anti-dumping duties. Moreover, the 
imposition of duties would not lead to a supply shortage to the user’s industry. 


The other interested parties have contended that Ministry of Textile had recommended 
withdrawal of anti-dumping duties because the acrylic yarn industry was suffering. The 
Authority notes that the documents in public domain relied on by the users merely state that 
the removal was recommended and it does not highlight the reason for such rejection. The 
duties were removed with an expectation that acrylic fibre would be available at 
internationally competitive prices. The report nowhere states that the yarn industry was 
adversely getting affected. It is seen that the users have not provided any information to 
establish the adverse impact of the past duties on the downstream industry. It has also not 
been shown how the removal of measures has benefited the downstream industry. 


The Authority also notes that the applicant has provided information on acrylic fibre prices 
in major consumption markets such as Turkey and China. It is seen that the prices in these 
markets are significantly higher than the prices in the Indian market. It is also seen that the 
demand for acrylic fibre has not recorded any material growth post the withdrawal of 
measures. It is therefore not established how the imposition of anti-dumping measures will 
result in Indian market prices becoming uncompetitive in comparison to international prices. 


POST DISCLOSURE COMMENTS 


Submissions by other interested parties 


The following comments to the disclosure statement have been filed by the other interested 

parties. 

a. Exclusion of Vardhaman Acrylic Limited required revised injury statement to be submitted by 
the applicant, and demonstration that the remaining applicant satisfies the standing 
requirement. 

b. Imports made by the related entity of Vardhman Acrylics Limited must be excluded from the 
analysis of volume effect as it leads to a typical case of self-inflicting injury. 

९. After the exclusion of Vardhman Acrylics Limited from the ambit of the domestic industry, 
the Authority is obligated to disclose such revisions to all interested parties in a timely manner 
and provide them with adequate opportunity for comments. 

d. ‘The Authority has considered Turkey under second methodology for determining the normal 
value is incorrect as the Authority failed to exhaust the first methodology of price or 
constructed value in the market economy third country.

i. While dumping and injury margin in case of Thailand and China PR is provided in the range 
of 0-50% and 0-20% respectively, the same is in the range of 0-!20% and 20-220% in case 
of Peru. Thereafter, cumulation of imports to do the injury analysis can give distorting results. 

i Subject product constitutes around 60-70% of the downstream users’ cost, and imposition of

projected in the disclosure.

o. Producer from Peru has fully cooperated with the Authority and denial of separate duty is

p. The Authority has not disclosed the basis or source of data used to determine the residual

producers can still import fibre duty-free under advance authorization. The removal of duties 
has not led to increased demand or improved performance in the downstream sector.

Examination by the Authority 


The Authority has examined the post-disclosure submissions made by the interested parties. 
It is observed that most of these submissions are reiterations of arguments and contentions 
that have already been examined and addressed to the extent deemed necessary in the 
relevant paragraphs of these final findings. The issues raised for the first time in the post 
disclosure comments/submissions by the interested parties and the domestic industry and 
considered relevant by the Authority are examined below. Any submission which was 
merely a reproduction of the previous submissions and which had been adequately examined 
by the Authority has not been repeated for the sake of brevity. 


The interested parties have commented that a cumulative assessment of injury is 
inappropriate for Thailand due to significant variations in the dumping and injury margins 
across the subject countries ranging from 0-5% for Thailand and China PR to 20-220% for 
Peru. The Authority notes that the goods imported from subject countries are technically and 
commercially substitutable with the like article produced by the domestic industry, and that 
consumers can use them interchangeably. The fact that the users have imported the product 
under consideration interchangeably within the subject countries establishes similarity in 
conditions of competition across the subject countries. Therefore, there is no need for a 
separate injury determination for imports from Thailand. 


On the comments of other interested parties on normal value determination, the Authority 
has already examined that the level of development of China and Thailand for the product 
under consideration are not comparable. While the interested parties claimed that level of 
development are similar, no evidence was provided in support of the contention during the 
entire investigation. The mere fact that installed capacity and production scale of the 
producer in Thailand is comparable with the participating producer from China, is not 
sufficient. While the data on record shows that the producer in Thailand is significantly 
export oriented, the same cannot be held for the producer in China as far as the product is 
concerned. Therefore, it cannot be considered based on the facts of the case presented by the 
interested parties, that Thailand is an appropriate market economy third country in the 
present investigation. It has been contended that the entire imports from Turkey cannot be 
considered for normal value as it contains both the product under consideration and the 
excluded product, it is clarified that the Authority has considered only the data for the 
product under consideration and on PCN basis based on DG System Transaction wise data. 


The interested partis have filed comments on the need for submitting the revised injury 
information by the applicants post exclusion of Vardhaman Acrylic Limited from the scope 
of the domestic industry, the Authority notes that the disclosure statement is based on revised 
injury data and the interested parties were given opportunity to defend their interests and 
make submissions. When Vardhaman Acrylic Limited has already been considered as 
ineligible domestic producer under Rule 2(b), the other two producers are the only eligible 
domestic producers and satisfy the requirement of standing. With regard to the allegation of 
self-inflicted injury due to the imports made by a related party of Vardhman, the Authority 
notes that any such possibility stands addressed by not including Vardhman as part of the 


[भाग aus ]] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 23 


52. 


53. 


54. 


L355. 


domestic industry. Injury assessment has been done by the Authority based on the 
parameters of the other two domestic producers which constitute domestic industry in terms 
or Rule 2(b). 


As regards the comment that the cyclic nature of demand confirms that the decline in profits 
in the period of investigation was natural and not due to alleged dumped imports, the 
Authority notes that while the demand had declined, the imports from the subject countries 
had increased. The increase in the imports from the subject countries was particularly driven 
by the fact that these imports were at dumped prices. The investigation has shown that the 
imports are below the cost and price of the domestic industry. Had these low-priced imports 
not increased from the subject countries, there was no reason for the domestic industry to 
reduce its prices. The increase in the imports below the cost of sales and selling price 
establishes the direct relationship between imports and injury to the domestic industry. 


With regard to the claim that the impact of duty on yarn makers and spinners would be 
higher ranging from 0% to 5, the Authority notes that acrylic fibre is an intermediate raw 
material used in manufacturing acrylic yarn, which in turn is used in downstream products 
such as apparel, blankets, and other textile applications. The applicants have contended that 
the demand for acrylic yarn closely aligns with that for acrylic fibre. Imports of acrylic yarn 
are negligible compared to domestic demand, with the highest import volume in the past 
decade reaching only 7,000 MT. The downstream users have not established that they do not 
pass on the increase or reduction in acrylic fibre price in their yarn prices. The assertion that 
the impact on yarn producers would be as high as 0-5% is neither supported by verified 
costing data nor any supporting documentary evidence. The Authority also notes that the 
imposition of anti-dumping duty is not aimed at increasing prices beyond non-injurious 
levels but only at offsetting the impact of unfair trade and restoring a level playing field. It is 
also noted that the Authority has found a significantly high dumping margin in the present 
investigation which shows that the producers in the subject countries are not exporting the 
product to the Indian market at global prices. 


On the submission that comparison made between prevailing price in India, China and 
Turkey is incorrect, the analysis has been made based on the data for the dedicated code 
which includes both basic fibre and the speciality fibres. The interested parties had also 
claimed that they are importing speciality fibres from China and Thailand. Therefore, it 
cannot be considered that the entire import into India of the dedicated code represents basic 
fibre. The data considered is on CIF basis which are on comparable basis for all countries. 
The Authority also clarifies that price difference in the Indian market and the other market is 
not the sole factor considered in arriving at the conclusions in the final findings. 


As regards the submissions on exclusion of sub-standard Acrylic Fibre from the scope of 
product under consideration, the Authority notes that the issue has already been examined in 
detail in disclosure statement and the Authority does not find it necessary to examine the 
issue in detail. The participating exporter from Peru has also not provided any new 
information and evidence. 


24 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.]] 


56. 


57. 


I58: 


I39. 


60. 


The interested parties have claimed that the Authority did not disclose the basis or source of 
data used to determine the residual dumping margins and that the reference to “best facts 
available” is insufficient. The Authority notes that the determination of residual dumping 
margins is in accordance with Rule 6(8) of the Anti-Dumping Rules. Where any interested 
party fails to provide the necessary information or impedes the investigation, the Authority is 
required to base its findings on the best facts available. The Authority had sent various 
communications to the producer seeking additional information/clarifications, and 
verification documents but the producer did not provide the relevant information and 
documents. Sudamericana De Fibras S.A. has failed to co-operate to the best of its ability. 
When the data of the producer could not be verified, the Authority had no option but to 
choose to apply facts available. 


As regards the comments on raw material procurement at higher prices and the decline in the 
raw material price in the period of investigation, the Authority notes that the applicants have 
provided information with respect to both (a) consumption price and (b) purchase price of 
acrylonitrile. It is seen that the difference between the two prices is only [***] %. It is also 
seen that the raw material cost reported by the applicant is i and the producer in Thailand are 
comparable. Therefore, the Authority considers that the past procurement of raw material at 
higher prices before market decline cannot be considered as a cause of injury. 


As regards the comment that there is difference in the import between the application and the 
disclosure statement, it is noted that whereas the applicant had relied on the market 
intelligence data in its application, for assessment of volume and value of imports, the 
Authority has considered the imports as per the DG System transaction wise data. Further, 
few product types have now been excluded. Therefore, the volume of imports and demand in 
India have undergone change as compared to the application. The data has also undergone a 
change because of the change in constituent of the domestic industry. Vardhman Acrylics 
Limited was included as domestic industry in the application, but the Authority has found it 
appropriate to exclude Vardhman Acrylics Limited in the scope of the domestic industry. 


The applicants filed certain comments on the determination of non-injurious price and have 
requested disclosure if their claims have been considered. It is clarified that the claims of the 
applicants with regard to notional customs duty on raw material imported under advance 
authorization, treatment of bank charges in relation to buyer credit and letters of credit, 
treatment of deposits in current assets, treatment of insurance income and calculation 
methodology for sales value ratio have been considered by the Authority. However, the 
claims of the applicants with regard to no normation of other raw material and utility costs 
and 5% profit for non-injurious price have not been considered. 


CONCLUSION 


Having regard to the contentions raised, information provided, and submissions made by the 
interested parties and facts available before the Authority, as recorded in the above findings, 
and based on above analysis of the dumping, injury and causal link to the domestic industry, 
the Authority concludes as follows: 


[भाग eve ]] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 25 


a.The product under consideration in the present investigation is acrylic fibre of all types and kinds, 
excluding homopolymer acrylic fibre, anti-pilling acrylic fibre, Bluesign® APPROVED gel- 
dyed acrylic fibre, pigment (or solution) dyed acrylic, high shrinkage acrylic fibre, pigment 
dyed or solution dyed acrylic fibre, recycled GRS-certified acrylic, acid-dyeable acrylic, anti- 
bacterial acrylic, thermoregulatory acrylic, and low-abrasion acrylic fibre containing !00% 
acrylonitrile 

b. The product under consideration is classifiable under Chapter 55 of the Custom Tariff Act, 
975 under sub-headings 550 30 00, 5503 30 00 and 5506 30 00. The product has also been 
imported under 55033090 as well. 

c.The product supplied by the applicants is a like article to the product under consideration imported 
from the subject countries. 

d. The imports made by Indian Acrylics Limited are not of such volume as to disqualify them 
from being treated as an eligible domestic industry. 

e.Vardhman Acrylic Limited has not been able to justify the reason for the imports made by its 
related entity and has not provided any information on whether the imported product has been 
consumed or sold in the domestic market or exported. Therefore, Authority has excluded the 
producer from the scope of the domestic industry. 

f. Indian Acrylic Limited and Pasupati Acrylon Limited constitute as domestic industry within the 
meaning of Rule 2(b) and satisfies the criteria of standing in terms of Rule 5(3) of the Rules. 


g. Sudamericana de Fibras S.A, the producer from Peru, has not offered verification of data, 
information, the Authority has not granted an individual dumping margin to the producer. 

h. It is found that the producers from the subject countries have exported the product to India at 
dumped prices. 


i. China was earlier a net importer of the product. However, with establishing significant capacities, 
China has transitioned from being net importer to net exporter. 

j. Producer from Thailand has significantly high capacities [***] MT as compared to the domestic 
demand [***] MT for the product. 

k. Despite the Indian industry have the capacity [***] MT to cater entire demand [***] MT and 
the demand for the product showing a decline in the period of investigation, the imports from 
the subject countries have increased by 9,235 MT. 

Il. The landed price of imports from subject countries is undercutting the prices of the domestic 
industry. The landed price has prevented the domestic industry to sell at remunerative prices 
and as a result, the domestic industry has suffered financial losses. 

m. The capacity with the domestic industry has remained constant throughout the investigation 
period. The capacity with the Indian industry is sufficient to cater entire demand in the 


country. 

n. With decline in the market share of the Indian industry, the production, domestic sales and 
capacity utilization of the domestic industry has materially declined in the period of 
investigation. 

0. The profit before tax and cash profit of the domestic industry has turned negative in the period 


of investigation. The profit before interest and tax and return on capital employed has sharply 
declined in the period of investigation. 


p. The investigation has not shown any other factor which could have caused injury to the 
domestic industry. The injury to the domestic industry is due to dumped imports. 

4. The imposition of anti-dumping duty will not have any adverse effect on the downstream 
industry. 


M. RECOMMENDATION 


26 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEC.]] 


l6l. 


62. 


The Authority notes that the investigation was initiated and notified to all interested parties 
and adequate opportunity was given to the domestic industry, exporters, importers, and other 
interested parties to provide positive information on the aspect of dumping, injury, causal 
link and impact of recommended measures. Having initiated and conducted the investigation 
into dumping, injury, and causal link in terms of provisions laid down under the anti- 
dumping rules, the Authority is of the view that imposition of anti-dumping duty is required 
to offset the dumping and injury. The Authority considers it necessary and recommends 
imposition of anti-dumping duty on imports of the subject goods from the subject countries. 


Having regard to the lesser duty rule followed by the Authority, the Authority recommends 
the imposition of an anti-dumping duty equal to the lesser of the margin of dumping and the 
margin of injury, so as to remove the injury to the domestic industry. Accordingly, the 
Authority recommends imposition of anti-dumping duty on the imports of the subject goods, 
originating in or exported from the subject countries for a period of 5 years from the date of 
notification to be issued in this regard by the Central Government, equal to the amount 
mentioned in Col. 7 of the duty table appended below.
